Verified with real rendered frames: the --locomotion-review footage for ours and the baseline, --onfoot-review, and --gameplay-photos. Headless only: the --test suite.. Judge: One side won all four pairs clearly, and none was indistinguishable. Each winning frame (pair 1 B; pair 2, 3 and 4 A) has three things the other lacks: dense, lived-in set dressing; hard, directional light that grounds people and props with crisp shadows; and staging, meaning a hero car or a story beat that frames the walking figure. Pair 2 A and pair 4 A genuinely wowed me. The losing frames, all from the same Singapore-set game, have good bones: believable skies and sun direction, a landmark skyline, rain trees, convincing shophouse and apartment-block facades, Peranakan tiles. They keep failing in the same ways. The plaza foregrounds are bare, with soft, blotchy tile textures. The main character is a stylized, low-detail avatar whose mid-stride poses look awkward when frozen in a still (a lunge, crossed legs on tiptoe). The background people are copies of the hero's model. Several surfaces are untextured blockout: the awning plane, the utility cabinets, and slabs that seem to float. Lighting on the characters is flat, with weak contact shadows. A small world-space bug: a street-name label runs past its sign plate. The fixes that would gain most, in order: dress and add detail to the ground plane, finish or replace the blockout geometry, add character variety and fidelity, and stage shots around a hero prop. Protocol note: 2 of the 8 images (pair-1 B and pair-3 A) carry a game-title logo in the corner, which reveals their source and breaks the blinding. All the winning frames also look like posed press stills, while the others look like raw gameplay captures. I judged only the rendered content, but the logos should be cropped or masked in future rounds. Composition comparisons are also tilted toward posed stills, though the gaps I listed above are real either way.

The in-development game has a clear identity: a Singapore-like harbour with landmark homages, a warm afternoon colour grade, several boats with white wakes, mirror-like reflections of the skyline in the water, and ripple rings from the rotor downwash. Its density at mid distance is reasonable. It loses consistently on six points. (1) The helicopter, the playable vehicle here, has a near-black, low-detail body, and its rotors render as dark translucent wedges or ellipses that look like artifacts. (2) The water is a uniform teal, with no colour change by depth, no transparency, no surf against the shore or structures, and foam that looks like flat blotches. (3) Materials are weak: boxy towers with repeating window grids, and piers, grass and park structures that are flat slabs. (4) The images are milky and low in contrast, without the layered haze that gives a sense of distance. (5) The camera either looks down from above or crops the subject, and a large HUD and empty minimap cover the skyline. (6) The character on foot has little detail and a stiff, arms-down idle pose. One caveat: the reference frames are hand-picked press shots with no HUD, which makes the gap look bigger. But even with the HUD removed, the in-development frames would lose every pair. These are still images, so they cannot show walking or running animation; only the stiff standing pose in pair 3 is visible. The fixes with the most effect, in order: rotors drawn as light motion blur instead of a dark disc, plus glossy helicopter materials (this is the playable vehicle); water that changes colour with depth and breaks into surf at the shore; haze that layers the distance; and a better-detailed, better-framed character on foot.

Its frames are staged marketing shots with a hero subject, so they have a composition advantage over the in-development game's HUD gameplay cameras. Even after discounting that, and the HUD and logo overlays, the in-development game loses on materials, density and believability. It has real strengths: an attractive backlit sky and haze, a recognisable Singapore-like identity (shophouses, rain-tree avenues, landmark skyline) and warm sun. Its failures repeat across frames. (1) The same blotchy, mottled asphalt with no aggregate grain or wear logic appears in pairs 1, 2 and 4, and it dominates the foreground. (2) Median planters of blocky low-poly plants appear in pairs 1, 2 and 4; in pair 2 they also intrude into the chase camera. (3) Placeholder or blockout elements show up as the sign on a beige box plinth, the bare-dirt lots along a 'city street', identical tiled-window towers and a lone hangar box. (4) Streets are empty of traffic and pedestrians, and the aerial view reads as a toy diorama. In priority order, the fixes are: rebuild the asphalt material, replace the median foliage, replace every dirt lot with built frontage, then populate the streets and roofs. Judge: The more polished image wins decisively in all four pairs. None of the losses is close, and none is indistinguishable. In three pairs (1-3) the winner is A; in pair 4 it is B. All four losing frames look like they come from the same in-development city game, a Singapore-like setting with a recurring olive-shirt protagonist. Its one consistent strength is warm low-sun lighting with long, soft shadows, and in places it does read as a real location (HDB blocks with void decks, shophouse colonnades). It loses for the same reasons every time. First, character fidelity: helmet-like hair, a waxy, expressionless face, flat cloth and a stiff default idle or gait. Second, life and density: empty plazas and streets with almost no pedestrians or traffic. Third, material and set-dressing sterility: one repeating paver texture dominates every frame, with no wear, clutter or props, and there are placeholder tells such as a blank sign and primitive blocks. Fourth, weak cinematic intent: the subject is centered or small, the compositions are static, and the landmark backdrops have no aerial perspective. The winning frames set a bar close to key art, with storytelling props, layered foreground-to-background depth, readable faces and crowded, busy streets. One of them carries a logo overlay, so they are curated marketing shots and not raw gameplay. The quickest gains for the in-development game would be populating streets with pedestrians and traffic, and upgrading the hero character's hair, skin and cloth shading. Breaking up the paver tiling and adding set dressing would come next. Only two winning frames genuinely wowed: the walk away from the burning truck and the crowded neon night street.

The B frames are clean beauty shots with no HUD and a publisher logo watermark, and the A frames show gameplay HUD; I discounted both overlays, and every verdict holds without them. The B frames get their quality from strong lighting design: a low sun or dusk key light, aerial haze that builds clear depth, and believable materials (gloss paint, glass, fabric, water spray). Their dynamic, staged compositions make the helicopter or the character the clear hero. The A frames come from one recognisable harbour city, and they do have a coherent palette, legible landmark massing, and boats with wakes that add some life. Every A frame shares the same problems. 1) Lighting is flat and mostly ambient, with weak value structure and little falloff with distance, so the city looks like a scale model. 2) The towers are boxy extrusions with one repeated window texture and no rooftop or facade variety. 3) The rotor is drawn as a solid ring or disc instead of motion-blurred blades. 4) The water has artifacts: smeared blur, pasted-on foam blotches, floating blur discs and streak reflections. 5) The foreground figure is mannequin-grade and staging is static. 6) Gameplay bugs are visible: pistol ammo shown while flying, an OS cursor in the minimap, a debug text line, and mission text starting in lower case. Suggested order of work: lighting and atmosphere first (directional sun with real shadows, plus height and distance fog), then rotor-blade rendering, then water foam and reflections, then facade detail and variety on the towers, and finally character fidelity. Only two B frames really impressed me: the dusk searchlight shot (pair 2) and the backlit banking helicopter (pair 1). No A frame did.

None of the four is close. Water shading is where the in-development game comes closest. It has good teal colour, normal-mapped ripples, clean planar reflections of the towers, and in pair 4 a sun-glint streak and several boat wakes. Two areas are far below AAA. First, how the water reacts to boats and the shore: spray is opaque white dot sprites, wakes are flat white bands with hard edges, foam uses an obvious Voronoi cell pattern, ripple rings are perfectly geometric, and hulls show no bow-wave displacement. Second, the world around the water: placeholder box buildings, cloned towers with identical window textures, flat untextured brown ground, sparse trees and traffic, no people, and empty horizons where the world visibly ends. Lighting is also flat, with no readable sun, long shadows, layered haze or lens effects, and the commercial frames rely on exactly those for atmosphere. The fixes that would help most are: (1) rebuild foam, wake and spray as turbulent, streaky, translucent effects that dissipate gradually, and (2) fill every land surface with varied, textured, populated geometry, adding atmospheric haze toward the horizon. The commercial frames in pairs 2, 3 and 4 genuinely wow. Pair 1 B is a competent but ordinary promotional shot. Methodology note: the commercial images in pairs 1 and 3 carry a visible game-logo watermark in the corner, so those two pairs are not truly blind. My verdicts would be the same without the logo, but the watermark should be cropped out of future comparison sets.

Placeholder elements recur too: flat cream or grey block facades, wireframe-looking landmark towers and blob shrubs. Asphalt is uniform and pristine, atmospheric depth is weak, and a heavy HUD with a blank-grid minimap covers the frame. The winning frames each beat this profile on a different axis. Pair 1 wins on crowd density and varied activity, pair 2 on light-and-material interaction and sky drama, pair 3 on surface wear and dynamic composition, and pair 4 on backlit aerial perspective. Only pair 2's B genuinely wowed me. Pair 4's A came close but is marred by blotchy dark specks across the sky. The fixes that would do most for the in-development frames, in order: more pedestrians doing more varied things, more vehicle and prop variety, worn ground surfaces, sharp reflections on cars and glass, and stronger haze and depth for late-day backlighting.

The B frames show a mature AAA pipeline: dense, specific dressing (signs, neon, street furniture, landmarks), excellent materials (car paint and chrome reflections, aged asphalt and kerbs), skies with real structure, strong aerial perspective, and deliberate cinematic cameras with a clear focal subject. Two of them, the pink-cloud dusk chase (pair 1) and the backlit biplane vista (pair 4), genuinely impress. The A frames all share one profile. On the positive side they have competent warm low-sun lighting with soft shadows, attractive rain-tree canopies, shophouse and Supertree landmarks that give a clear sense of place, and believable hero-car paint. Against that they reuse placeholder-grade assets in every shot: the white box bus with a purple stripe, smooth green blob shrubs, block buildings with flat decal windows, and Supertrees that read as wireframes. They also show untextured or empty asphalt and plazas filling large parts of the frame, flat gradient skies with no clouds or haze band, few and idle pedestrians, a static default gameplay camera with HUD clutter, and occasional clipping bugs (an NPC hand and a cropped vehicle in the frame corners). In priority order, the A fixes are: replace the placeholder vehicles, shrubs and facades with fully modelled assets; fill sidewalks and junctions with active, varied pedestrians, signals, crosswalk markings and kerbside props; give the asphalt grain, wear and a specular response; and add a cloud-structured sky, horizon haze and backlit atmosphere. None of the A frames wows me.

They win on four points: a dense, believable world all the way to the horizon; directional sunlight with real atmospheric perspective (backlit glitter, haze layering, lens flare); rich, varied materials; and a hero subject with dynamic simulation (turbulent foam wakes, crashing spray, rotor blur). Life is sparse: few boats, no visible people, empty roads. The water simulation is stylized: wakes are thin straight streaks, whitecaps are uniform noise even in a sheltered harbour, and quay foam is a constant white line. The strengths of A are the near-field water shading (normal detail, long tower reflections, sun sparkle), a recognisable landmark layout, a clean HUD, and a decent Ferris wheel. To compete, the priorities in order are: populate and detail the environment out to the horizon and remove placeholder geometry; establish a real sun with atmospheric depth; replace the streak wakes and noise whitecaps with foam that responds to boats and structures; and give hero vehicles motion blur and spray. None of the A frames wows. Three of the four B frames do (lighthouse, helicopter, train); the speedboat promo is strong but more ordinary.

Lighting is mostly ambient with weak or missing sun shadows, except pair 4, where a warm low sun casts believable long shadows. Distant skylines have almost no haze to show distance, so near and far buildings have the same contrast. Roads carry almost no traffic and there are almost no people, and large flat ground areas are left empty. The HUD is placeholder (an empty grid minimap with a visible mouse pointer). The commercial frames win on three things the in-development game does not try yet: depth from sunlit haze, life in every layer (traffic, aircraft, people), and deliberate composition (foreground framing, dutch angles, dusk or backlight). The most valuable fixes, in order: a directional sun with shadows plus distance haze; enough traffic and pedestrians to fill the roads; material detail on roads and ground; water that reflects sky and skyline; and more varied building facades. Pair 4 shows the ground-level renderer is getting close on light; what is missing there is mainly life and surface detail.

The B frames show the same arid Southern-California world: dense, weathered and full of story, with layered aerial haze and lighting that its geometry backs up. Long cast shadows, contact shadows, sky reflections in car paint and rim-lit silhouettes make the sun position believable. The A frames are a Singapore-themed city with shophouses, supertrees and a Marina-Bay-style hotel, and they share the same weaknesses across all four. Large surfaces are clean or untextured: asphalt is a flat dark plane, paving repeats perfectly and roofs are flat single colours. Population and prop density are very low. Landmarks look like placeholders (wireframe-looking supertrees, cloned extruded towers). There are artifacts: hollow ring-shaped tree canopies from above, a pure-black median face, and a bare flat horizon where the world seems to end. The lighting gets the warm colour grade, bloom and some haze right, but it has few shadow-casting objects in the foreground, so it never shows the sun direction the way B does. A's best assets are its warm golden-hour grade and the ornate shophouse facades with lit interiors. The fastest wins would be material variation and wear on asphalt and paving, street furniture and vehicles that cast long shadows, more pedestrians, and replacing the greybox roofs and clone towers. None of the A frames wows. Two B frames genuinely do: pair 1's weathered liquor-store hero shot and pair 2's flare-lit, backlit desert inn. Pair 3's flat midday grass and pair 4's smudgy clouds keep those B frames just short. Note that the pair-2 B image carries a visible game-title logo, which breaks the blinding; my verdicts are based only on what is in the images.
